A classification answers within the control channel's deadline only because the director's session is bounded to 60 seconds; a slower model or effort ends as `unrecognized` rather than waiting. A classification session cannot be reopened: its directory is removed when it ends. Workflows change only on the account's coordinator; a computer signed in to one reads them as synchronized. The Settings tab list has eight categories since Settings › Workflows, so on a narrower desktop it wraps onto a second row.

Delivery destinations are proposed from the CLI or MCP only. The GUI approves them, delivers and
removes them, but has no form to create one. There is no GitHub Actions secret adapter yet: it needs
a libsodium sealed box. The delivery rate limits are in memory and reset when the host restarts.

Ordinary shared accounts support API-backed assistant operations. SQL sources that could
use OS-integrated authentication require a personal trusted machine. Their embeddings
requests reuse public-source DNS/IP pinning and reject operator-local destinations.
Request/child creation, Git clone/sync and configurable CLI providers on the shared
server remain unavailable to ordinary accounts. Profile folders are not OS isolation.


- **Account store link.** The Access service token is per account and lasts a year (renewed in Cloudflare). Replacing it rotates its secret (`cloud_token`) and the account's computers trusted with the account store since 0.10.38 receive it from their coordinator within a minute; one trusted earlier, or without the account store, is trusted again with it. A computer that is not signed in to a coordinator (a second standalone installation) is given a new token by hand (`cloud connect`). A token with another client ID moves the account with `rebind` and is delivered the same way, but `ACCOUNT_SHARES` must then be updated. Per-account shares of the plan's quota are set only in the Worker's configuration (`ACCOUNT_SHARES`, keyed by service token client ID); they are ceilings, not reservations, and `cloud status` says when every account at its share would exceed the plan.
- **Account configuration.** Synchronization is whole-document with per-item patches, not a merge: two clients editing at once get a conflict for the later one, resolved by synchronizing and repeating. A profile configured before signing in must be adopted with `reset` (a private backup is kept); there is no field-by-field merge of local and account settings. People profiles, legacy cases, the Graph chat allowlist, policy sender allowlists, activity settings, knowledge-index settings and launchers are not synchronized yet. More than 2,048 notes or a document over 900 kB is refused. Granting a source access, including enabling or widening it, is done on the coordinator, not from a connected installation. The Activities screen still reads this installation's own record; the coordinator's saved days are reachable with `account_records`/`pta account records`. Legacy migration is a separate slice.
- **Account readiness.** `setup_status` reports each step from the operations that own it and never acts; the optional Teams and version steps are never required for `ready`, and `ready` says nothing about Teams having received or sent a message (see the counts). The directory choice it points to is the shared directory sheet in Settings › Execution machines; there is no second selector. The real-host CLI/MCP flow of the setup path is covered on Unix like the other host-process tests; on Windows the report's own unit tests run in CI but the whole path against a real Windows host, a sign-in with a second client and the update are exercised by their own slices' tests, not by one end-to-end Windows run.
- **Start at login.** A Windows task at logon runs in the user's interactive session only, and a Linux unit only while the user manager runs (lingering keeps it after logout). A host that a backup restore restarts on macOS or Windows starts its successor itself, which then runs outside the entry until the next login or update (a systemd unit restarts it). A host installed as an entry from a host `pta` started does not move under the manager until the next login or update. For an isolated profile (`PTA_PROFILE_DIR`), the host `pta` starts runs with the profile's private home and without the session's variables, so `pta service` refuses there (`not_ready`): start `personal-teams-assistant --host` from the session with `PTA_PROFILE_DIR` set first.
- **Updates and rollback.** `pta update apply` installs a prepared pair and rolls back an unhealthy one, but only for installations the helper can restart itself or through a start-at-login entry: a host under a hand-written systemd unit (the shared server's), a development build or a non-crates.io install updates the way it was installed. It does not retract work only the new version could read after a rollback, and the safety copy needs free disk space. The check sees only crates.io's sparse index, so a mirror that replaces it in the user's Cargo configuration is not consulted for discovery. A staged build needs the Rust toolchain: the in-app update always compiles the crates.io source and never downloads the prebuilt archives (those exist for `cargo binstall`). An installation made with `cargo binstall` has no record of its own in Cargo's `.crates2.json`, so it is updated with `cargo binstall` again (or reinstalled with `cargo install` to use the in-app update).
- **Variable consumers.** Supported consumers are exactly the DeepSeek API key and owner-only local launchers. A running assistant keeps the DeepSeek key it read at start until it restarts. Only the first slot of the registry exists; further built-in consumers are added in code. Launchers are proposed, approved and run from the CLI/MCP of an owner's trusted machine, and also approved on the owner's local page or their own portal session (the local page lists, approves and removes them; the portal lists and approves); approval on the local channel rests on the confirmation the CLI and MCP ask for, which an agent with the owner's `pta` can give; their output is deliberately not captured, and an approved program can retain what it receives (rotate the external credential if a program is no longer trusted). Redaction is never relied on. Machine-local launcher files and bindings are not yet synchronized or migrated. Which variable a consumer or launcher uses is enforced on the trusted machine, not by the coordinator: `variable_resolve` serves any entry of the account to a trusted, unrevoked machine of that account, one audited entry at a time. Trusting a machine grants it the account, so a stolen machine credential is handled by revoking the machine (every later resolve is then refused) and rotating the affected values at their providers; a coordinator-side allowlist registered by the same credential would not stop its thief.
- A new execution machine joins through browser sign-in and one Trust this machine confirmation (0.9.12+, both installation and coordinator), or through the operator-local CLI and a private single-use pairing file. A pending sign-in lives in host memory: restarting the host abandons it and the coordinator request expires unused. The unauthenticated start endpoint is bounded and rate limited but can be crowded by anyone who knows the portal address; the user then retries after a minute. Approval pages show the confirmation code; users must compare it, because approving a stranger's request (consent phishing) would add that computer to their account. Individual machine revocation is available from any connected account client (0.9.13+); it denies future access but cannot retract information already given to an external process. Stored variables exist per account ([Account variables](#account-variables)); their consumers (DeepSeek key, owner-only local launchers) re-run this live account check at each use, and no machine credential reaches them. The portal supports one owner per installation. Profiles and provider working-directory permissions are not OS/container isolation and must not be offered to unrelated tenants.
- The SQL Server adapter uses Tiberius 0.13 with patched Rustls, and Access tests use a public synthetic DER signing fixture instead of the vulnerable RSA test dependency.

- Maps saved with `questions`, URL groups or pinned Wiki page URLs require host/CLI version 0.9.0 or later. A map with a work item guide that sets `listed_with_bugs` requires 0.10.15 or later; until a guide sets it, To-Do lists no task-category type with the bugs. A map with an `[organization]` section or an `organization` source requires 0.10.16 or later, and so does every installation of an account whose portable configuration carries the organization or guides (an older one rejects the document and reports an error until it is updated). An installation that kept work item guides of its own before 0.10.16 shows unsaved local changes once it updates; the user saves them on the coordinator or synchronizes with reset (a backup is kept). A map or account document with workflows or workflow blocks requires 0.12.0 or later: once the coordinator saves the first one, every installation of the account older than 0.12.0 rejects the account's configuration (`deny_unknown_fields`) and stops receiving its changes, reporting an error, until it is updated. Likewise, a map or account document whose workflow settings differ from the defaults (`[workflow_settings]`: ready states, the watcher, concurrency, the pull-request wait or a director) requires 0.12.1 or later, and every installation older than 0.12.1 rejects the account's configuration once the coordinator saves them; a map that keeps an edited `direct` prompt (`[[agent_prompts]]`, which stays on each computer) requires 0.12.1 to load. Enrolled machines must be updated before they can run Teams with the new question configuration.

- Workflow runs (`implementation/workflows.db`, §16) are read with `deny_unknown_fields`, so a run that holds a send record of a progress summary (`summary_sends`, written since 0.12.6) cannot be read by an older host: after a rollback below 0.12.6 such a run fails to load and the older host's run operations and engine report it as unreadable until the host is updated again. Runs without a send, and every summary kept in `summaries` (a field that existed since 0.12.3), load as before.
- Implementation machines need explicit enrollment with the user's HTTPS portal before presence is discovered across networks. The coordinator must remain reachable. The host on each machine must support `implementation_support`; no additional server or package is installed. Coding runs are fully autonomous (§16): no provider sandbox confines them (Claude `bypassPermissions`, Codex `danger-full-access`, OpenCode allow-all), so an agent can run any command, read and write anything the machine's OS user can, use the network, commit, push and open pull requests; the selected directories and allowed roots choose where it starts, not an OS isolation boundary. The confinement that remains is the owner's trusted machine, its allowed roots, the person's choice of directories, CLI and model, the restricted child environment and PTA's own confirmations; merging, publishing and deploying are left to the person by the prompt's fixed rules, not by a sandbox. A prompt a CLI still raises (explicit ask or deny rules, organization policies, Claude's critical-path `rm` checks) is recorded in the run (`permission_prompt`) rather than answered. Claude Code refuses `bypassPermissions` when the host runs as root or under `sudo` on Linux/macOS, and an administrator can disable it in managed settings: such a run fails at start. The child environment does not carry `SSH_AUTH_SOCK`, so a push over SSH works only with keys usable without the agent; HTTPS remotes use the CLI's or Git's own credential helper. OpenCode requires V2. Isolated portal profiles are restricted to their own `data/repositories` roots.
- Implementation history persists every run in SQLite, lists up to 100 matching runs within an 800 KB response budget (`partial` reports older results omitted; retrieve a run by ID) and bounds an active run to two hours. The card/detail session index shows up to three summaries per item, prioritizing active executions. Interrupted runs are never resumed automatically. Machine calls time out after 40 seconds and are not requeued after a lost claim; inspect runs before starting another attempt. Child creation has no automatic reconciliation after an uncertain write; its recorded links and provider state need human review.

- To-Do GitHub epics are issues of type Epic, with native open/closed states. Project-board custom
  workflow states and dates are not read; unavailable issue start dates remain null. The GitHub
  App must already have Issues read access. Detail images/attachments remain original links,
  without inline image previews; process form extensions and GitHub Project-board layouts are
  not reproduced. Large discussions/histories report their documented bounds. Azure To-Do stays within the configured catalog and
  team projects; it does not widen the source to unrelated organization projects.

- One Teams identity per profile. Team channels are not supported.
- Daily task records are local snapshots, refreshed by schedule or on request. Task detail reads current Azure fields; a saved daily list retains its original day. Large/old historical reads may exceed the candidate/revision deadline and retain the previous snapshot (§6.11).
- The host must keep running for scheduled snapshots; their result appears in the GUI's Activities tab, with no notification. No call or chat discovery is involved in daily recording.
- Ended with the window (0.11.0): the tray and menu-bar icon (its status dot and start/stop item), the native notifications of the daily record and the native notification of a failed tray operation. The assistant's state and start/stop are on the GUI's Home and `pta start|stop`, and the daily record's result on its Activities tab. Web Push for the installed PWA is a separate proposal.
- Reception needs the computer on and a stable HTTPS URL to the listener (Cloudflare tunnel with a token, an own `cloudflared` file or an external tunnel).
- A normal answer reads one tool (it does not combine a Wiki search with activity); activity reviews combine sources, and status rounds add only the user's own Wiki pages of the last month (pages by other people, such as a validation page, are not read). Writing or sending an answer from the app needs the assistant running.
- An activity review takes about two minutes with Codex at medium effort (about half a minute of Azure DevOps reads, the answer and the reference selection), and a busy period may hit its bounds (60 commits, 15 pull requests, 10 runs, 10 releases, 10 approvals, 12 Wiki pages and 40 messages); it says so when coverage is partial. Approvals depend on what the Release API returns for the account.
- Teams documents that inline styles in messages sent through Graph "may not function as intended": if a client ignores them, colored text shows in the default color (the word is still there). Table borders and heading sizes depend on the Teams client.
- Question recognition uses model semantics even without embeddings. Broader mistakes described in negative ratings depend on the model following corrections; code blocks identical rejected repeats. A person is recognized by Teams ID or exact name only.
- A repository source reads Markdown and text files only (no code), at most 5000 per repository; a context is matched by its words, not by meaning, so a request that names a subject only through a synonym missing from its keywords is answered without it. Semantic search needs an external or local embeddings endpoint; the configured models offer none.
- `repository_docs` reads only root README/OpenAPI files of repositories named like the topic; it does not search code (validators, DTOs) or folders, and a repository whose name does not carry the topic words is not found.
- `pta chat` returns the answer as unconverted Markdown (the GUI renders it). On the local page, links in answers are copied; they do not open in the browser (the web portal opens them in a new tab). The local test chat reads the own Teams messages source with the host's Microsoft session; a token obtained while the assistant is stopped lasts about an hour, so a longer simulation reports that source unread.
- Chat reads (`teams messages|search`) read at most 1000 messages of one chat per call (then a cursor), and across chats the 40 with the latest messages in the window with 500 messages each; search reads 200 Microsoft Search hits. Microsoft Search ranks by its own index, which can lag new messages and does not search channels, and some tenants refuse it for chat messages with `Chat.Read` (HTTP 403, seen on the production tenant on 2026-11-05), so the direct reading runs there; it matches whole words only and its `next_cursor` pages older matches. Messages in Team channels are not read.
- The GUI does not create tool sources (except the activity record) or edit their parameters (`tool`, including the Azure DevOps catalog) or `allowed_senders`: use `pta sources add`/`sources edit`/`pta config set`.
- `missed` recovery covers only the recent page; there is no guarantee of processing messages during outages.
- The audit (`assistant.db` › `jobs`) has no retention: every received message stays, so the file grows with use. `audit list` pages it (`audit_list`, `Store::inspect_page`: at most 1000 entries or about 1.5 MB per page, `next_cursor` the last resource, ordered by reception second and then resource so the cursor misses no tie). The GUI's Messages tab still shows the latest 100. A group message without a mention keeps no text in the audit (`ineligible_message`); `teams messages RESOURCE` reads it live, while the chat is readable and the message exists.
- It does not detect whether the user answered manually while the proposal was being written.
- The context history comes from the chat's recent page (50 messages); the test chat (`pta chat`/GUI) has no history. In chats with other people, answers sent by the app show as `me` (only the personal chat marks them as `assistant`).
- Without a model's selection, a Wiki answer lists every consulted page, even if it did not use them all.
- With maximum reasoning an answer can take several minutes, and the worker processes one message at a time: the next ones wait in the queue. If the default provider fails slowly (e.g. a CLI that hits its 20-minute deadline), the fallback adds that wait.
- OpenCode requires version 2. Its GUI model dropdown offers only available TUI favorites from the host’s OpenCode state, with no free-text IDs; missing/unreadable preferences or metadata offer no models. Mark favorites in OpenCode on the host, then use Detect CLIs again. Effort offers `default` plus only that model’s reported variants; changing the model resets an unsupported effort to `default`. A saved non-favorite or unsupported effort remains in the profile and is shown as a notice, never inserted into the options or silently replaced. Without favorites, adding an OpenCode row is disabled. Discovery and calls use the same isolated configuration, so project/user customizations are not loaded. A failed session cleanup makes the call fail, and an abrupt host exit can leave an OpenCode session in its own database. The Claude and DeepSeek models in the catalog are a fixed list (their CLI/API publish no local catalog); other valid IDs are set with `pta config set llm.chain`. The Codex/Claude/OpenCode CLIs are not tested on Windows (looked up as `.exe`).
- Windows MSVC host builds reserve 8 MiB of main-thread stack at link time (`/STACK:8388608`), with the default commit size; `pta`, non-MSVC Windows and other OS targets keep their defaults. In 0.9.8, Windows startup overflowed the default 1 MiB stack (`0xc00000fd`); changing only the reserve to 8 MiB allowed startup. The stack-consuming function has not been identified and infinite recursion has not been established. The host's runtime runs on the main thread. See [Microsoft's /STACK reference](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/cpp/build/reference/stack-stack-allocations?view=msvc-170).
- Windows CI performs a clean release Cargo installation and checks the host's and CLI's PE stack fields and subsystems (the host is a GUI-subsystem program, so it opens no console; `pta` is a console program). Then, twice (without flags, then with `--headless` and `PTA_HEADLESS=1`, both ignored), it starts the headless host on a disposable account, checks its version and state with Teams stopped and no visible window, checks that `pta app open` answers `not_ready` without a browser session and never returns a code, and that launching the executable again leaves the running host in place, then quits and relaunches it (`scripts/test-windows-startup.py`); it then runs clippy and selected account, machine, variable and update tests. This full check runs on pull requests that touch a platform-sensitive path and weekly, and is skipped on pushes to `main` to avoid repeating the clean release build. The Windows job remains informative; the full Rust test suite does not run there, and the smoke check does not cover an existing user's profile or every Windows configuration.
- Cargo has no post-install hooks: the previous host keeps answering with the old version until the first `pta` or opening the new app.
- The version published on crates.io may lag the repository; GUI and CLI must come from the same build (the CLI rejects Wiki schemas against a host without `wiki_support`). Profiles and knowledge maps only move forward: a file saved with a field a newer version added is refused by an older host or CLI (`deny_unknown_fields`), while older files keep loading.
- `llm.language` covers the GUI, Teams and activity drafts, not the CLI, audit log, step names or diagnostics (English), and the configured greeting (`policy.greeting`) stays as written. Host errors outside the GUI catalog, and parts of provider or Azure DevOps error details, are shown in English. Model-written drafts follow the language as well as the model does. Detection without a model covers Spanish and English phrases; other phrasings go to the model's intent classification.

## 16. Work item implementation and execution machines

`app/implementation.rs` owns durable implementation runs, reviewed child creation and canonical directory checks; `app/implementation/store.rs` records executions and native sessions in SQLite. `app/implementation/fleet.rs` owns enrollment, presence, one-shot remote calls and the Teams lease; `app/implementation/login.rs` owns the installation side of browser-assisted sign-in. Both are host operations shared by the GUI, CLI tools and MCP. Machine settings and child-send intents live in private `implementation/registry.json`; every agent execution is recorded in `implementation/runs.db`, with indexed work item identity/link, provider/model/effort, machine, native session ID, state and request/start/finish timestamps. Durable update events replicate session summaries into the coordinator's SQLite index, so an offline machine retains its last reported sessions; the account's existing profile identity, credential names and data directory are unchanged. `implementation/connection.json` contains the coordinator origin, account and machine identity; `PTA_MACHINE_TOKEN` is kept in the existing credential store. Snapshots never expose it. Hosts and CLI capabilities advertise additive `implementation_support`.

To-Do cards and shared details place Implement before the original-link action. HUs/bugs and GitHub epics produce a bounded, validated child plan using the chosen machine's installed CLI and selected model/effort. The user edits and selects children, then confirms creation. Tasks (including process-defined custom task types, such as a development bug listed with the bugs) and ordinary GitHub issues launch coding directly. GitHub's All issues filter and `get_github_issue` support ordinary issues, and verified sub-issues can open shared detail. Provider type/category verification happens on a fresh read before starting or creating anything; IDs, URLs and source scope are not supplied by a model. The Implement form of an Azure item whose project has an enabled workflow adds a **Workflow** select (`implementation.js`): *No workflow* (the form as before), *The director chooses* and each enabled workflow of the item's project. Choosing a workflow fills and locks the machine, directories, agent, model, effort and context (with a note naming the workflow; to choose them, start without one); *The director chooses* sends `implementation_start {item, workflow: "director"}` at once and shows the director's choice and reason (or that it chose none, with Start disabled) before anything starts, and Start then sends the chosen workflow's ID, which opens a workflow run (`workflow_run_start`'s path). The started run's state shows inline with a Refresh (`workflow_run_status`) and a link to the Workflows section; the plan review is still the Implementation runs list's create form until the Runs views arrive. See §16 Workflows.

The implementation form selects context, execution machine, multiple canonical directories, an installed Codex/Claude/OpenCode CLI, model and supported effort. Changing machines clears directory/model selections. Its directory browser lists only permitted roots and directories, rejecting symlink escapes; isolated portal profiles can use only their own repositories. A run records its intent before launching, rejects overlapping working directories and has a stable UUID to avoid repeated remote starts. Cancellation terminates the CLI process tree. Startup marks in-flight runs interrupted and pending creations uncertain; neither is replayed.

All GUI directory choices use the same modal directory sheet (a bottom sheet on phones), including remote implementation directories, this machine's allowed roots and local checkout add/edit paths. A `../` row starts each open directory's list. Selections are staged across parent/child navigation and applied explicitly; Cancel/Escape discards them. `implementation_browse` remains confined to the selected execution machine's saved roots. Read-only `machine_browse` lists this host's filesystem roots for configuring roots and local checkouts, independently of saved execution roots: the single root `/` on Unix; on Windows every fixed local drive (`C:\`, `D:\`, …, read with `GetLogicalDrives`/`GetDriveTypeW` from `kernel32`, no extra dependency; removable, network, CD and RAM drives are left out, and the home drive is the fallback when none can be read), so a directory on any fixed drive can be browsed and saved as a root; isolated web profiles remain confined to their own `data_dir/repositories`. Both use the same canonical directory listing and reject symlink escapes. Browsing never changes roots or grants execution access; roots still require `machine_save`, and existing portal isolation checks still govern checkout changes. The portal address is entered in the sign-in screen (§11) on the computer to enroll, never on the coordinator itself; the last validated origin is remembered and prefilled (`machine_settings.coordinator_origin`). In the browser GUI the coordinator never offers Connect this machine.

`llm/implementation.rs` is separate from the tool-less answer transports. Planning uses a closed JSON interface in its own persistent, tool-less CLI session; coding runs the selected CLI in the first directory with additional directories passed through provider controls. Prompts travel on stdin, the inherited environment is restricted to the existing CLI/login/proxy allowlist, output is bounded and redacted, and no assistant Graph/ADO credentials enter the child environment. The CLI's own login remains in force.

**Fully autonomous coding (0.11.10).** A coding run (a task, development bug or issue) runs the agent with full permissions on the execution machine and never stops for a person (decision in §14); planning and request runs keep their read-only, tool-less settings. `llm::implementation::permission_mode` names the mode each provider is launched with, and the run record stores it:

| Provider | Coding launch | Documented meaning | Planning/requests |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Claude Code | `claude -p --input-format stream-json --output-format stream-json --permission-mode bypassPermissions --disallowedTools AskUserQuestion` (plus `--safe-mode --strict-mcp-config`) | `bypassPermissions` (equal to `--dangerously-skip-permissions`) "disables permission prompts and safety checks so tool calls execute immediately"; in `-p` "the few calls that would still prompt are denied instead" (explicit ask rules, critical-path `rm`, `AskUserQuestion`); deny rules and `permissions.disableBypassPermissionsMode` in managed settings still apply; it refuses to start as root or under `sudo` on Linux/macOS. A bare tool name in `--disallowedTools` removes the tool. ([permission modes](https://code.claude.com/docs/en/permission-modes), [CLI reference](https://code.claude.com/docs/en/cli-reference), [headless](https://code.claude.com/docs/en/headless)) | `--permission-mode acceptEdits --tools ""` |
| Codex | daemon `thread/start` with `sandbox: "danger-full-access"`, `approvalPolicy: "never"`; `codex exec --sandbox danger-full-access -c approval_policy="never"` when the daemon is unavailable | `danger-full-access`: "No restrictions whatsoever" (no sandbox; `workspace-write` blocks the network by default and keeps `.git` read-only, which stopped commits and pushes); `never`: "Never ask the user to approve commands. Failures are immediately returned to the model". The same effect as `--dangerously-bypass-approvals-and-sandbox` ("Skip all confirmation prompts and execute commands without sandboxing"). ([`SandboxPolicy`, `AskForApproval`](https://github.com/openai/codex/blob/main/codex-rs/protocol/src/protocol.rs), [CLI options](https://github.com/openai/codex/blob/main/codex-rs/utils/cli/src/shared_options.rs), [reference](https://developers.openai.com/codex/cli/reference/)) | `read-only`, shell/MCP/web disabled |
| OpenCode V2 | `opencode run --standalone --agent=pta-implementation` with `OPENCODE_CONFIG_CONTENT` giving that agent `[{"action":"*","resource":"*","effect":"allow"},{"action":"question","resource":"*","effect":"deny"}]` | "The last matching rule wins"; agent rules are appended after global and project rules, so the agent's allow-all wins over an `ask` elsewhere; `deny` blocks; a `question` nobody can answer is denied. Organization (global or Console-managed) policies can still hard-deny. A non-interactive run rejects a request that still asks ("permission requested: …; auto-rejecting"). ([permissions](https://opencode.ai/v2/docs/permissions/), [policies](https://github.com/anomalyco/opencode/blob/HEAD/services/site/src/docs/content/policies.mdx), [non-interactive `run`](https://github.com/anomalyco/opencode/blob/HEAD/packages/cli/src/run/noninteractive.ts)) | every action denied |

`--permission-prompts none` (Claude Code 2.1.259+) and `opencode run --auto` are not passed: an older CLI rejects an unknown flag, and the modes above already resolve every prompt. Nothing in a launch reads a terminal: the prompt goes on stdin, output is JSON on stdout, Claude shows no bypass dialog in `-p`, so a run started from the portal on a computer nobody is sitting at (the headless host) needs no one there.

**A prompt that still happens.** No run ends in `needs_attention` because of a permission prompt. If a CLI still raises one, the run records its exact text and goes on: Claude's `permission_denials` in the result and any `can_use_tool`/`elicitation` control request during the delegated turn (answered at once with a refusal, even when Remote Control is connected; after the turn the retained connection belongs to the person's app again), Codex daemon requests ending in `/requestApproval`, `/requestUserInput` or `/elicitation/request` for the run's thread (refused at once), and OpenCode's `permission.asked` events and its "permission requested:" line on stderr. At most 8 prompts of 2000 characters each are kept, redacted like the rest of the output, in the run's `prompts`; a run with any recorded prompt ends in `permission_prompt` (shown as stopped on a permission, with the prompt text, so its cause can be removed on that computer), whether or not the agent then finished.

**What still confines a run.** The fixed coding rules (below) forbid work item writes, Teams sends, merging, publishing and deploying; the agent may branch, commit, push and open pull requests. With these modes no provider sandbox confines a coding run any more: the confinement that remains is the trusted execution machine of the owner the person chose, its allowed roots (the working directories must be inside them; the agent can still reach anything the OS user can), the restricted child environment (no assistant Graph or Azure DevOps credential, the existing allowlist), PTA's own tools and confirmations for an agent reaching it through MCP, and the shared server's refusal to run coding for other accounts (`authority::require_execution`). See «Known limits».

The run record (`implementation/runs.db`, payload) adds `permission_mode` (`bypassPermissions`, `danger-full-access`, `allow-all`, or `read-only` for planning; absent in a record from an older host, shown as not recorded) and `prompts`; `implementation_runs` returns both, and the run detail shows them. Session summaries replicated to the coordinator are unchanged, because older coordinators reject unknown fields.

**Connect this computer (0.11.4).** Settings › Machines in the portal shows the [one-command installation](#one-command-installation) for the visitor's operating system (detected in the browser, macOS, Linux and Windows selectable) with a copy action. The command comes from `machine_join_command` (read-only tool, in the portal allowlist, offered to the remote agent): the portal answers it itself in `web::forward` from its own public HTTPS origin and `CARGO_PKG_REPOSITORY` (`implementation/join.rs`), never from the account host's settings, and refuses without an HTTPS public URL; on a computer connected to a coordinator the host builds the same command for that coordinator. The crate embeds no maintainer hostname. Running it opens the coordinator's approval page on the new computer; the request appears there with its code for the existing Trust this machine. Pending requests are deliberately not listed in the computers list: the start endpoint is unauthenticated and not bound to an account, so a list would put anyone's request in front of every account.

Browser-assisted sign-in (0.9.12) is the normal enrollment path; `app/implementation/login.rs` is the installation side and `app/web/enrollment.rs` the coordinator side. `machine_login` validates the coordinator's HTTPS origin (no credentials, path, query or redirects), remembers it in private `implementation/installation.json` and posts `login_start` with the machine ID, name, OS, the SHA-256 of a private installation key kept in that file, and the SHA-256 challenge of a random verifier that stays in host memory. The coordinator stores the request in private `web/machine-logins.json` (locked, at most 100, ten-minute expiry, rate limited per machine and globally) and returns a 256-bit public transaction handle and a short confirmation code; the installation builds the approval URL `ORIGIN/login?enroll=HANDLE` itself and opens it in the system browser (headless hosts and agents only return it). GUI, CLI and MCP see only the origin, URL, code, expiry and state. `/login` and `/` keep a valid `enroll` handle across Access and local-session redirects; the shared GUI then shows a Trust this machine dialog naming the computer, OS, code and signed-in account. Its `inspect`/`trust`/`deny` calls use POST `/api/enrollment`, which requires the Access assertion, the local session, exact Origin and CSRF. A decision is single and binds the request to the session's account ID and version; a request started by an installation already connected to an account carries that account's provider subject and only that account can see or trust it. Opening or signing into the portal never creates a machine. `machine_login_status`/`login_status` reads the state; `machine_login_finish`/`login_finish` presents the verifier and machine ID, and only an approved request whose account is still admitted at the same version is consumed (before any host is contacted) and forwarded as `machine_register` with `enrollment: "login"` through the existing account-bound machine transport. Wrong verifiers or machine IDs are refused; replayed, expired, cancelled, denied and revoked requests issue nothing. The account host refuses a sign-in that claims an enrolled machine ID with a different installation hash (operator pairing files may still replace it), so repeating sign-in from the same installation rotates its own credential without duplicates and another installation cannot clone it. The installation rechecks the account, stores the returned credential as `PTA_MACHINE_TOKEN` in its own credential store and only then writes `connection.json`; any failure leaves an existing connection unchanged. A finish returns `authorization_pending` until approval. Trust grants no roots, Teams machine, assistant start or provider login. **Account store at trust time (0.10.36, decision 18).** `login_start` also carries `store_key`, the public half of the installation's own X25519 key pair (`mirror::handoff::Receiver::machine`: created on the first sign-in, kept in `keys/cloud-machine.json`, 0600, and reused while the computer stays trusted; until 0.10.37 it lived in memory for one sign-in); the confirmation code then covers it (`handoff::code`: the SHA-256 of the handle and the key), and the installation shows the code it computes itself, so a key swapped in transit shows another code on the approval page (a coordinator without the handoff answers the handle's code and the sign-in goes on without it). The Trust this machine dialog offers «Also give it the account store» when the request has a key and the account's profile keeps a content key and a link to the store (`store_available`); only that tick in that session sets `store` on the approval. The consumed request then forwards `store_key` and `handoff` (the handle) to `machine_register`, which records `store_key` on the machine (the key the person approved, so later deliveries go to it) and seals `{endpoint, client_id, client_secret, token_expires_at, content_key}` to that key (`cloud::handoff`: an ephemeral X25519 key, HKDF-SHA256 salted with the domain over the handle and both public keys, AES-256-GCM-SIV with a random nonce, the same values as associated data), or says why not (`unavailable`). The portal, the tunnel and Cloudflare carry only that ciphertext. After storing its machine credential the installation opens it with the sign-in's key and installs it (`cloud::receive`): the content key only when it has none (a different one is never replaced), the Access service token in its credential store, then `cloud_connect` enrolls it in the store with the mode unchanged (nothing is uploaded); it reports its store installation to the coordinator (machine protocol action `store`), kept as the machine's `store_installation`. `machine_revoke` then also revokes that installation in the store (`store.revoked`) and says what else a lost computer needs (`store.next`): the revoked computer keeps the content key and the service token, so replace the service token (`cloud_token`) and rotate the content key; the account's other computers receive both (`store_delivery`, §3). CLI: `pta auth machine login [URL] [--no-browser]|status|finish [--wait]|cancel`. The Access bypass for `/api/machines` covers these unauthenticated `login_*` actions, which never accept a bearer token; `/api/enrollment` stays behind Access. The flow follows the device-authorization pattern of [RFC 8628](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8628) and the native-app guidance of [RFC 8252](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8252); it does not change the Teams OAuth flow.

The portal's exact POST `/api/machines` endpoint is separate from authenticated browser control. Pairing-file enrollment requires a single-use, ten-minute, machine-bound pairing credential authorized locally on that installation with `pta web machines authorize MACHINE_ID [USER] --output PRIVATE_FILE`. The command writes a 0600 JSON file for `machine_connect` on stdin and persists only a hash under the private portal directory. Consumption is locked and persisted before contacting the host, and checks explicit account admission, account version, expiry and machine ID. GitHub tokens never authorize enrollment and are never sent to a coordinator. No account is created. Enrollment issues a random machine credential and stores only its hash, bound to the account version. All subsequent calls are authenticated with it; re-association, disabling an account or disconnecting the machine revokes access. Browser Origin requests, cookies without a machine credential and other methods/paths do not authorize machine operations. `configure-web-access.py` installs one exact Access bypass for this machine protocol; the backend still authenticates every request, and `/api/control` remains Access/session/CSRF-protected. Workers poll outbound, so the Mac needs no listener reachable from the Internet.

A coordinator defaults to itself as the Teams machine. Every service start requires a lease for the selected machine; renewal runs separately from implementation polling every ten seconds. Handover blocks the new machine until the old 120-second lease expires. An existing service stops if it loses renewal or is deselected. The updater also checks implementation runs and child sends before restarting a host. This coordination covers enrolled hosts of the same account; an independent, unconnected installation is not discoverable.

Machine management (0.9.13+, `machine_management_support` in the host descriptor and CLI capabilities) uses the coordinator-authoritative registry from every account client. `machine_list`/`machine_detail` expose only that account/version's names, OS, trust, online/unknown/offline presence, last-seen time, last-known assistant state, allowed roots, management/directory/assistant capabilities, the running `version` and the `update` state. Worker polls persist last-known state; older workers have unknown capabilities until upgraded. A poll's additive `host_version` (kept only when it is a release number) and `update` (`state` one of `current`, `available`, `staging`, `staged`, `deferred`, `applying`, `unsupported`; `pending` and `failed` release numbers; `unattended`; anything else is dropped) are shown as reported and read as unknown (`null`) from older workers; the coordinator's own row reports itself, and a shared portal account's host reports nothing. They are display only, and the version gates of the coordinator (too old to join or to keep the account store consistent) are unchanged. The one update another device can ask for is [`machine_upgrade`](#updating-to-the-newest-release-in-one-request) (`capabilities.upgrade`, from the poll's additive `upgrade_support`): it names only the machine, never a version, package, address or command, and that computer runs its own update to the newest release; no other update operation is reachable through the worker protocol or the portal. No machine credential/hash, installation key or control token enters these results. `machine_update` edits names centrally, including offline machines. Root edits and `machine_directories` run once on the selected online compatible target; both GUIs reuse `chooseDirectories()`. Root writes canonicalize existing absolute directories and reject symbolic-link aliases (on Windows, junctions too, including one that points to another drive). Paths shown to people and stored as roots use the plain spelling: the Windows verbatim prefix of canonical paths (`\\?\C:\…`, `\\?\UNC\…`) is removed from listings (`machine_browse`, `machine_directories`, `implementation_browse`), from `machine_detail`/`machine_list` roots and from what a root write stores (`implementation/paths.rs`). Checks canonicalize again, so both spellings are accepted; roots saved with the prefix by older hosts read back plain when the registry loads, and a coordinator normalizes the roots a worker reports, whatever its own operating system. Successful browser sign-in and private-file pairing clear previous local root grants before switching the connection; pending/denied attempts preserve them. Empty native roots grant no execution access (there is no home-directory fallback); isolated portal browsing/execution retains its repository confinement, and the portal operator still controls its roots.

`machine_revoke` works for selected/offline remote machines: persist a tombstone, clear its credential hash/root metadata, remove queued calls and deselect it without starting a successor. Authentication, remote dispatch, Teams selection and lease renewal reject revoked machines. Preserve the prior lease expiry under a retired holder identity so even a fresh enrollment of the same installation must wait for handover. A host connected as a worker rejects coordinator protocol requests, so credentials it issued in its former coordinator role cannot proxy into the newly connected account. Only new browser trust or operator-local pairing can issue a new credential; a revoked credential cannot enroll. The coordinator cannot revoke itself through account operations. Revocation does not retract data an external program already received. A timeout never replays an action; GUI controls refresh the authoritative list before another deliberate attempt, and agents must inspect list/detail first. CLI and MCP share the same catalog operations and confirmation requirements.

Both Settings machine lists expose the existing `machine_teams` choice as radios, preserving the saved default. Each row adds a small Start/Stop button backed by the shared `machine_assistant` operation (`machine`, `action: start|stop`; CLI/MCP confirmation required). It forwards once through the account-bound worker protocol and invokes the ordinary `start_assistant`/`stop_assistant` dispatcher on the target, preserving ownership/lease, activity and login guards. Selecting a radio does not start the service or move the coordinator's portal/data. Start is offered only for the selected, online, stopped machine; Stop remains available for an online running machine during handover. Worker polls publish additive `assistant_phase` (`stopped`, `starting`, `running`, `stopping`); a missing report from an older worker is unknown and disables its control, and offline reports are labeled last-known. Assistant commands run concurrently with the worker's presence/status polling so Graph readiness cannot make the machine disappear. Timeout or failure refreshes the list without replaying the command. Directory/name edits survive ownership and service controls.

Child creation is a new operation, independent of activity registration and its legacy write adapters. Azure uses the enabled source catalog/read credential to discover the process's default task type, preserves parent area/iteration and assigns the child to the catalog user. Azure CLI obtains a current Entra token for Azure DevOps and the host sends it as a Bearer credential; no separate write PAT is used. GitHub uses the existing connection with Issues write access to create and link same-repository sub-issues. Each plan is marked sending before its first write and cannot be submitted twice. A GitHub issue whose parent link fails is recorded as created_unlinked with its verified URL, and further children stop. A transport/readback failure is uncertain and never retried. Existing daily snapshots and all normal evidence tools remain read-only.

The coordinator owns the child-creation ledger, including submissions from enrolled workers. After validation and a durable sending intent, creation proceeds in the background. `implementation_creation` reads its outcome and verified child links. A repeated submission returns the recorded outcome without issuing new provider writes; an uncertain outcome is never replayed.

Native agent session IDs are streamed from Codex thread events, Claude session events and OpenCode session events; they are distinct from PTA run UUIDs. An execution is recorded as started only after its CLI process starts or the shared daemon accepts its thread; its native ID becomes available as soon as the provider reports it. Successful coding ends in `done`, with a finish timestamp. Native sessions are retained for inspection. An interrupted session has no fabricated completion time. Cards and details read the session index, and shared detail shows provider, native ID, state and start/end times. Codex desktop links use the verified `codex://threads/<thread-id>` format ([official reference](https://learn.chatgpt.com/docs/reference/commands)); local CLI sessions are not assumed to exist in mobile/cloud apps. Mobile browsers offer the provider connection sheet; unsupported providers show a reason. The native opener accepts only a recorded session on that host, never an arbitrary custom URL.

Implementation sessions also attempt provider-owned mobile access by default, independently of whether PTA is a browser tab or an installed PWA. Codex starts/reuses its local Remote Control daemon and runs the thread/turn through that daemon's local WebSocket control socket (Unix socket or Windows named pipe). This gives the mobile client the same live conversation, rather than an `exec` thread omitted from the default interactive listing. Planning/request threads retain read-only, tool-less settings: shell and integration features are disabled, each configured MCP server is explicitly disabled, and the response uses the closed JSON schema. If a compatible daemon cannot be reached before creating a thread, execution can fall back to the existing CLI with mobile access marked unavailable; a started thread/turn is never replayed. Cancellation interrupts the daemon's turn as well as terminating ordinary CLI processes.

Claude uses its stream-JSON control protocol to initialize and enable Remote Control before sending the initial user turn. Its provider-returned `https://claude.ai/code/session_…` ID is stored separately from the local UUID; local IDs are never substituted into cloud URLs. After the initial result, the process can keep serving the provider's mobile client for up to two hours from connection, with at most 16 retained connections per host. Subsequent work in that provider conversation does not rewrite the completed PTA implementation run. Missing eligibility or connection failure does not authorize extra tools or fail an otherwise valid initial run. The ordinary tool-less Teams answer transports stay ephemeral and do not enable remote access.

`implementation_mobile_session` (`machine`, recorded run `id`, `action: connect|pair|close`, `confirmed: true`) routes once to the owning execution machine, rechecks the saved working directories against current roots, and reconnects completed Claude sessions without sending another model prompt. Running Claude sessions reuse their reported connection and cannot be resumed concurrently. Connection metadata is additive in run payloads and replicated session summaries. The authenticated response for Codex pairing contains only its short-lived manual code and expiry; pairing credentials are never saved in runs, the index or logs. Closing a retained Claude connection does not erase its transcript; Codex's machine-wide daemon is managed in Codex.

The mobile session sheet uses explicit user-clicked native and HTTPS links, preserving the gesture iOS requires when leaving a PWA. Claude uses its verified remote URL, the documented `claude://code/<remote-session-id>` native route and a separate web fallback. Older `exec` transcripts are resumed and, when still classified as non-interactive, forked once through the native protocol into an interactive mobile conversation; its real remote thread ID is saved separately and reused, preserving the original session. Codex exposes the connected computer, remote session ID and on-demand pairing code, then directs the user to that computer's conversation in the ChatGPT mobile app; there is no fabricated direct mobile URL for a local Codex UUID. See [Codex Remote Control commands](https://learn.chatgpt.com/docs/developer-commands), [Codex app-server protocol](https://learn.chatgpt.com/docs/app-server), [Claude Remote Control](https://code.claude.com/docs/en/remote-control), and [Claude mobile links](https://support.claude.com/en/articles/14898120-open-the-claude-mobile-app-with-a-link).

For a completed local Claude session, native PTA can use the official `claude --desktop --resume SESSION_ID` handoff on supported macOS/x64 Windows hosts. It does not move an active CLI session. Missing/unsupported desktop apps, CLI versions or subscription logins return a reason; browser/mobile sessions still need a verified remote URL. See [Claude CLI desktop handoff](https://code.claude.com/docs/en/desktop#cli-to-desktop).

### Agent instructions and work item guides

`agent_prompts.rs` holds the four prompts the implementation agents start from: `plan` (children of an HU, bug or GitHub epic), `code` (a task, development bug or issue), `request` (a new HU, bug or epic) and `direct` (the workflow director choosing a workflow for a work item, [Workflows](#workflows)). Their default texts live in `llm/prompts.rs`; `[[agent_prompts]]` in the map keeps only what the user changed (`id`, `instructions`, at most 8000 characters), and the snapshot carries the built-ins for editors. `llm/implementation.rs` always appends the fixed rules (untrusted evidence, guides and organization entries, no provider writes; for coding: nobody can answer during the run, and no work item or issue writes, Teams sends, merge, publish or deploy) and the JSON contract, so no edit can remove them. The built-in `code` prompt states the authorization (the person chose the trusted computer, directories, agent and model and confirmed the start) and the expectation: finish the request, including branch, commits, push, pull request and tests when it asks for them; when a step would need a person, decide, continue and report the decision; never wait for an answer. The host that starts a run resolves the instructions and sends them in `Launch.instructions`; a worker that predates the field ignores it and uses its own defaults.

`work_items.rs` defines `[[work_item_guides]]`: `id`, `provider` (`azure`/`github`), `type` (the provider's exact type name), `name`, `projects` (empty applies to all; one naming the project wins over a general one), `purpose`, `instructions` and `fields` (`reference`, `name`, `required`, `values`, `default`, `guidance`, `fill`), and `listed_with_bugs` (Azure only: To-Do lists that task-category type with the bugs, for a process that classifies its development bug as a task; without it To-Do lists only the requirement and bug categories). At most 50 guides and 80 fields each; `fill` is accepted only for Azure fields outside `System.*`. A guide is the organization's data: the repository names no organization's types, fields or states. A guide reaches the agents as untrusted input with today's date: every guide of the parent's project when planning (`Launch.guides`), the item's own guide when coding, and the destination type's guide when writing a request. MCP exposes them (`work_item_guides_list`) and its instructions tell an agent to read the guide before it creates or changes a work item by any means. The guides are the organization's `work_item_guide` entries (§8) and travel with the account (§3).

The organization's norms, architecture and conventions that apply to the project (the parent's when planning, the item's when coding, the destination's for a request) reach the agents as untrusted input too (`organization` in the input; `Launch.organization` for runs on another machine, ignored by an older worker): those naming the project first, then the general ones, each whole while their texts fit in 24000 characters (`organization::for_agents`). The fixed rules call guides and organization entries untrusted evidence; neither authorizes a destination, type, field or value.

The plan and request schemas add `fields` as `{reference, value}` pairs (strict for every provider), and plan children add `type`. A child type must belong to the project's `Microsoft.TaskCategory` (empty uses the default). A field is written only when the guide for that type and project marks it `fill`, the type's live definition (`workitemtypes/{type}/fields?$expand=allowedValues` plus the organization's field kinds) has it writable and not an identity, and the value converts to its kind and, for a closed list, matches an allowed value; an emptied value is skipped. `implementation_create` checks every child this way (`ado::implementation::check_child`, read-only) before the sending intent, and request generation checks the draft's fields before `sending`; a field error therefore writes nothing. The plan review shows each child's type and proposed values (a list for guide values) for editing before confirmation. GitHub ignores types and fields.

### Workflows

A **workflow** ([CONTEXT](../CONTEXT.md)) is a saved recipe for implementing the items of one Azure DevOps project; a **workflow block** is a reusable part of it. The definitions and their operations exist since 0.12.0, the workflow settings, the `direct` prompt and the director's dry-run classification since 0.12.1, the GUI's Workflows section and Settings › Workflows (§11) since 0.12.2, workflow runs with their engine and operations since 0.12.3, the director's progress summaries and their sending to Teams since 0.12.6, and the watcher (automatic starts) with the workflow choice in Implement since 0.12.7; the acting blocks since 0.12.8, the GUI's Runs view (§11) since 0.12.9, and the end-to-end rehearsal with synthetic providers (`app/workflows/rehearsal.rs`) since 0.12.10.

**Data.** `workflows.rs` defines `[[workflows]]` and `[[workflow_blocks]]` in the map (`#[serde(default)]`, left out while empty, so an older host keeps loading the map). A workflow has `id`, `name`, `description` (what the director reads to choose it), `enabled`, `auto_start`, `organization` (`https://dev.azure.com/NAME`), `project`, `repositories` (`{id, name}` from the project's Git catalog), `machine` (an execution machine of the account; the coordinator's own machine ID for the coordinator), `checkouts` (repository ID → canonical directory on that machine, one for every repository, each different), `agent` (`provider` `codex`/`claude`/`opencode`, `model`, `effort`), `instructions` (≤ 8000 characters), `blocks` (ordered, unique, existing block IDs) and `updated_at` (set by the host). A block has `id`, `name`, `kind` and only its kind's fields: `instruction` has `text` (≤ 8000) and no stage; `teams_notification` has a `stage`, `chat` (`id` as `pta teams chats` lists it, `name`), `mentions` (≤ 20 member display names of at most 100 characters and no `]`, which is what a Teams mention `@[Name]` can carry) and `template` (≤ 4000; only the placeholders `{item}`, `{link}`, `{pull_requests}`, `{summary}`); `launcher` has a `stage` and `launcher` (`id`, the program's 64-hex `digest`). Stages: `before_planning`, `plan_ready`, `children_created`, `child_done`, `pull_requests_completed`, `done`, `failed`. Limits: 50 workflows, 100 blocks, 20 repositories and 10 blocks per workflow. `KnowledgeMap::parse` checks all of it, so a hand edit, an import or a restored record is refused the same way. The definitions travel in the account's portable configuration (§3) and the account store's map record, but change only on the coordinator, where their runs will live: `Patch` carries no workflow field, every workflow operation on a computer signed in to a coordinator answers `not_ready` and names the coordinator, and `save_settings` (the GUI's settings editors, `config apply|set`, the map-editing tools) keeps the stored workflows and blocks whatever its map holds, like it keeps `[cloud]`. The workflow operations commit through the same settings commit (`commit_settings`: validation, the account store's write-through in primary mode, the journaled commit) under the operations lock, from the map as it is at that moment.

**Repository catalog.** `workflow_repositories` (`project`, optional `organization` and `source`) reads `GET {organization}/{project}/_apis/git/repositories` (`ado/repositories.rs`) with the enabled Azure source's read credential, after checking that the project is one of the accessible projects of the source's configured organizations (`request_targets`, as explicit requests do); a name found in several organizations needs `organization`. The reply is cached five minutes in `todo-cache.db` under the credential's fingerprint, like the process metadata, leaves disabled repositories out, and fails explicitly above 1000 repositories instead of returning part of them. It only reads: no source, audience or credential changes.

**`checkout_inspect`.** An additive one-shot machine call (`app/implementation/checkout.rs`) through the existing worker protocol (`fleet::call`, never reachable as a coordinator `call`). One call carries every checkout of a workflow (`paths`, at most 20) and answers one fixed state per path, in order: `outside`, `missing`, `not_checkout`, or `checkout` with the canonical `directory`, `link` (the path as written goes through a symbolic link below its root) and the `origin` URL of `[remote "origin"]`. A path that is not, as written and component by component, a root or below one is `outside` before anything on disk is read, so the answer never tells whether a directory elsewhere exists; a path below a root that resolves outside the roots is `outside` too. It runs no Git: it reads `.git` (a directory, or a `gitdir:` file with its `commondir` for worktrees) and the repository `config`, each only when it resolves inside the roots, bounded to 256 KB; an HTTPS origin loses its user and password before it leaves the machine. Workers advertise it with `checkout_inspect_support` in their poll (kept on the machine record); an older worker is never asked and reads as unknown, as does an offline one or one that does not answer.

**Saving a workflow.** `workflow_save {workflow}` first applies the map's own rules (limits, instructions, block IDs) so nothing is read for a definition the map would refuse, then gathers the facts and checks them (`workflows::check`): the machine must be one of the account's (and the coordinator itself requires execution authority: the shared server runs no coding agent for another account); the project must be in the source's configured organizations (its spelling is taken from the catalog); every repository must be in the catalog with its catalog name; every checkout must be an absolute directory as its machine spells it (the machine's reported OS: `C:\…`, `C:/…` or `\\server\share\…` for Windows, `/…` elsewhere, whatever system the coordinator runs; `workflows::absolute_on`), keyed once per repository (checkout keys are compared and stored in lowercase, so the same repository in two letter cases is refused), and is inspected in that single call: inside the roots, not reached through a symbolic link below its root (refused, like a root containing a link), a Git checkout, and, when it has an origin, one that names that repository (`workflows::azure_remote` reads the `dev.azure.com`, `visualstudio.com` and SSH spellings; an origin naming another repository or another host is refused); the agent's provider, model and effort must be installed on the machine (`implementation_catalog`, asked together with the inspection). A checkout the machine spells otherwise without a link (letter case on a case-insensitive file system) is saved as the machine's canonical directory, and a warning says so. An unknown project is refused before the machine is asked anything. The catalog read is bounded to 60 seconds and the machine calls run together (each at most 40 seconds), so the save answers within the control channel's 120-second client deadline and never commits after the client gave up. What cannot be verified is a warning in the reply rather than a refusal: an offline or older machine (its checkouts are then checked only against the roots it last reported, component by component in its own spelling, without letter case on Windows and macOS), a checkout without a readable origin, an agent catalog the machine did not return. `workflow_save {id, enabled}` changes only `enabled`. `workflow_remove` needs confirmation and is refused while the workflow has a run that is not done or cancelled. `workflow_block_remove` is refused while a workflow names the block.

**Blocks that act are authorized when saved** (epic decision 7). Saving a `teams_notification` or `launcher` block needs confirmation (`confirmed`, the MCP client's question or the CLI's `--confirmed`; an instruction block needs none on any surface, and the catalog asks by the block's kind), and both the refusal and the reply show what it does (the stage, chat, mentions and template, or the launcher and digest). A notification's chat must be one the user belongs to: it is checked against the user's chat list (`Graph::chat_directory`, the list `pta teams chats` shows; the personal chat `48:notes` is always the user's) through the running assistant's or the profile's Microsoft session, and refused when the list does not hold it; when no session can read it on the coordinator, the block is saved with a warning. Mentions are not checked at save: they are resolved against that chat's members when the block posts. Saving a `launcher` block also takes the owner's authority, exactly as approving a launcher does: the owner at this computer's local page (`Transport::Window`) or in their own portal session (`Transport::Browser`); the local channel (CLI, `pta mcp`), the remote agent endpoint, machine credentials and shared portal accounts are refused. It also needs a launcher of this installation (the coordinator) that is approved with that exact digest; a launcher that is missing, not approved or approved with another digest is refused. Running either block belongs to the engine slice and will recheck the chat, the members and the launcher's approval at that time.

**Settings** (`workflows/director.rs`). `[workflow_settings]` in the map (`#[serde(default)]`; the map's `[[workflows]]` key already names the definitions, so the settings cannot be a `[workflows]` table) holds `watch_interval_minutes` (1 to 60, default 5), `concurrency` (workflow runs that advance at once, 1 to 4, default 2), `pull_request_wait_days` (1 to 60, default 7), `director` (`machine`, `provider` `codex`/`claude`/`opencode`, `model`, `effort`; none until chosen) and `ready_states` (`organization`, `project`, `states`: at most 50 projects with 1 to 20 different states each). The section is left out while every value is its default, so a map without it loads with the defaults and is written without it until a value changes. `KnowledgeMap::parse` checks the bounds and shapes. The settings travel in the portable document and the account store's map record like the workflows, change only on the coordinator (`Patch` carries none; `save_settings` keeps the stored ones; the update answers `not_ready` on a computer signed in to a coordinator), and the watcher and engine of later slices read them. `workflow_settings` returns every value with the defaults, the bounds and `editable`; with `project` (and `organization` or `source` when needed) it adds `process_states`: the workflow states of that project's requirement, bug and listed task types (the types To-Do lists without a filter, `listed_with_bugs` included), read from the process with the read credential and cached like the process metadata (`ado::todo::process_states`). `workflow_settings_update` changes only the fields given and is checked before anything is saved. `director` gives the fields to change, merged with the stored director (a new director needs `machine`, `provider`, `model` and `effort`, and the refusal names the missing ones); `director: null` clears it. Each `ready_states` entry replaces that project's states, as the catalog resolves the project (a name in several organizations needs `organization`); an entry without states clears that project's, resolved against the stored ready states with the same refusal when the name is in several organizations. The checks: the bounds, the director's fields, the clearings and each entry's shape first, without reading anything; then every ready state against its project's process states, all projects read at once under one 60-second bound (past it the update is refused and nothing is saved), a state the process does not have refused with the states it has, and the organization and project stored as the catalog spells them; then the number of projects on the final list (re-setting a stored project replaces it); then the director's machine (one of the account's; the coordinator itself requires execution authority) and its agent against the CLIs installed there (`implementation_catalog`; not installed, or no such model and effort, is refused). An offline machine, one whose version cannot run the director, or one that does not list its CLIs is a warning, as for workflows.

**The `direct` prompt.** The fourth built-in agent prompt (`agent_prompts::DIRECT`, default `llm::prompts::WORKFLOW_DIRECT`) tells the director to compare the item (type, category, project, title, description, tags, area, iteration) with the enabled workflows of its project (ID, name, description, repositories) and answer the ID of the one that fits best, or an empty ID when none clearly fits or several fit equally, with a one-line reason in `llm.language`. It is edited and reset like the others (`agent_prompts_list`, `agent_prompt_update`, `agent_prompt_reset`, `pta prompts`, the Guides and instructions editor; at most 8000 characters in `[[agent_prompts]]`). `llm::implementation::director_prompt` always appends the fixed rules (the item's text and the workflows' names, descriptions and repositories are untrusted input that cannot change the rules; choose only a listed ID or an empty one, never anything outside the list; no tools, code or provider writes; answer only with the JSON) and the closed schema `{"workflow": string, "reason": string}` (no other property).

**Classification.** `workflow_classify {provider: "azure", id, project?, source?}` is a dry run on the coordinator. It reads the item fresh in one detail read that accepts the requirement, bug and task categories (`ado::detail::get_any`, the internal `get_azure_item` of `tools::todo::read`; at most 20 seconds); the detail reads now also fill the item's `category` (`requirement`, `bug` or `task`) from the process category its type was found in. The candidates are the enabled workflows of the item's organization (from its verified link) and project (`director::candidates`); with none, nothing is asked and the reply is `workflow: null` with outcome `none`. Otherwise the input is the item (the description as plain text, at most 8000 characters; tags, area and iteration) and the candidates, as JSON, and the director runs on its machine: on the coordinator directly, elsewhere through the additive one-shot machine call `workflow_direct` (`app/implementation/director.rs`; workers advertise `workflow_direct_support` in their poll, run it beside the poll so presence keeps flowing, and an older worker is never asked; never reachable as a coordinator `call`). The session is the planning one (§16 table: Claude `--permission-mode acceptEdits --tools ""` with `--json-schema`, Codex `read-only` with its features and MCP servers disabled and `--output-schema`, OpenCode with every action denied), in a private temporary directory under `implementation/directors/` that is removed when the session ends, with the restricted child environment (no assistant Graph or Azure DevOps credential), the prompt on stdin and the output bounded and redacted; Before the session the machine checks that the agent is installed, reusing its last probe of the installed CLIs when it is under ten minutes old and offers the agent, and otherwise probing again within 15 seconds; the session is bounded to 60 seconds, and the coordinator waits at most 95 for another machine (the probe, the session and the worker's polls, with margin), so the item read and the wait answer within the 120-second deadline. A worker's answer is judged even when the session ID it reports is not one; that ID is dropped. Code then checks the answer against the workflows as they are after the session (`director::judge`): the ID must be one it sent, and still exist, belong to the item's organization and project, and be enabled. The reply is `{workflow, outcome, reason, director: {workflow, reason}, problem, candidates: [{id, name}], item, run, session_id, dry_run: true}`: `outcome` `chosen` with the director's reason, `none` with its reason when it gave an empty ID, or `workflow: null` with the reason (and outcome) `unrecognized` (an ID it was not sent or that does not exist, an answer that is not exactly the JSON asked for, an empty answer, no answer in time), `other_project` or `disabled`. Nothing starts and nothing is written to a provider or the map.

**The session record.** Each classification is recorded on the coordinator as an agent session in `implementation/runs.db` `request_runs` with `purpose: "classification"`: the director's machine, provider, model, effort, `permission_mode` `read-only`, the native session ID (streamed live on the coordinator, reported at the end by a worker), request, start and finish times, the classified item with its verified link, and `classification` (code's verdict, what the director said, the candidate IDs). It ends `done` when the director gave a JSON answer and `failed` otherwise (with a fixed reason in `output`; a worker's own text never reaches the record), so `implementation_history` (Agent history) lists it and `implementation_request_status` reads it; startup marks one still generating `interrupted`, and the updater defers while one runs. Its session cannot be reopened from PTA: its directory no longer exists.

**Workflow runs** (`app/workflows/record.rs`). A **workflow run** is one execution of a workflow for one work item, kept only on the coordinator in `implementation/workflows.db` (SQLite, WAL, next to `runs.db`). Table `workflow_runs`: `id` (a stable UUID), `workflow_id`, `item_provider`, `item_id`, `item_url` (the verified link), `state`, `created_at`, `updated_at` and `payload`, the whole record as JSON; indexes on the item, the workflow and the state, and a partial unique index on `item_url` for every state but `done` and `cancelled`, so an item has at most one active run even under concurrent starts. The record holds the workflow's ID and a snapshot of its definition and blocks taken when the run got its workflow (a later edit, disable or removal does not change a run), the item (`provider` `azure`, ID, project, organization, verified link, title, type, `category`, state, source), `state` and `stopped_in` (the stage a stopped run stopped in), the `timeline` (`stage`, `started_at`, `finished_at`, `outcome`, one entry per stage entered; a resume adds the stage again), the director's `classification`, the `planning` run and each child's `coding` run (`id`, `machine`, `started`, the state last observed), the creation `ledger` entry (the planning run's ID, as `implementation_create` keys it), the `children` (ID, title, verified link, type, the workflow repository and checkout it is coded in, the coding run, start and finish, the reported `branch`, its `pull_requests` with ID, repository, title, status, verified link, dates and `accepted`, and `outcome` `done` or `accepted`), `block_outcomes`, `summaries` (filled by a later slice), `attention` (what the user must look at) and `error`, plus the engine's bookkeeping (`admitted`, `waiting_since`, `checked_at`, `creation_accepted`).

States: `classifying` (the director chooses), `unassigned` (no workflow: the director chose none or there is no director; `workflow_run_assign` gives one), `planning`, `plan_review` (the plan waits for the user), `creating_children` (the ledger is sending), `coding`, `pull_requests`, `blocks`, `done`, `failed` (the engine could not continue: a launch refused for a reason other than an offline or busy machine, a missing definition), `cancelled`, `interrupted` (the host restarted while an implementation run of this run was active) and `needs_attention` (with `attention` naming the cause and the linked run or pull request). `done` and `cancelled` are final; `failed`, `interrupted` and `needs_attention` wait for `workflow_run_resume`. The runs waiting on the user (`plan_review`, `unassigned`, `needs_attention`, `interrupted`, `failed`) are the attention items: `workflow_runs` returns them as `attention`, and the snapshot (`workflow_attention`) and the MCP `status` tool carry them: Home lists them once in its «Workflow runs waiting for you» panel (§11).

**The engine** (`app/workflows/engine.rs`) is a tracked task of the control loop beside the daily scheduler: every 30 seconds it looks at the runs in the stages it drives and advances each as far as it goes, up to 16 steps, each run in its own task: a tick waits for its steps at most until the next tick, a run still advancing then (a long director session) keeps going and is skipped until it ends, so a slow run never holds the others. It runs only where `may_run` holds: the account's coordinator (no coordinator link) with the owner's authority, so never on a computer signed in to a coordinator and never in a shared portal account that is not the owner's. It never starts a provider process itself: it asks for implementation runs with the same `implementation_launch` call `implementation_start` makes (`implementation::launch_planned`, with the same instructions, guides and organization entries; `build_launch` is shared), observes them with `implementation_runs`, reads the creation ledger, and reads Azure DevOps with the enabled source's read credential; it never calls `implementation_create`, writes a work item, or merges, completes or changes a pull request. Each step is durable before it acts: the ID of an implementation run is recorded with `started: false` before the machine is asked to launch it, and `implementation_launch` is idempotent by run ID (a machine that holds that run answers `existing` and starts nothing), so a restart between the two asks for the same run again and never starts a second one. A launch refused because the machine is offline or another run uses those directories (two runs of one workflow share its checkouts) is waited through with the reason in `error`. Every change of one run holds that run's lock (the engine's step, a resume, a cancel, an assignment); the director's session is waited for without it, and its verdict applies only to a run still classifying, checked against the workflows as they are after the session (one removed or disabled meanwhile is not assigned and the run is `unassigned`).
